# lean/Laws.lean

> THE LAWS BEHIND THE COUNTS — the closed forms this ledger has been sealing one value at a time. Wave.lean carries ten families of per-modulus counts — divisors, units, idempotents, nilpotents, the unit sum, the largest unit order — 334 theorems in all, each stating a NUMBER and no reason for it. Every one is generated by a closed form in the factorisation of m: d(m) = prod (e+1); phi(m) = prod p^(e-1)(p-1); the idempotents number 2^omega(m), blind to multiplicity; the nilpotents number m / rad(m); the units sum to 0 for every m > 2. Measured against the ledger's own sealed values: 334 counts, 334 reproduced, zero exceptions. NOTHING IS DEPRECATED. Those 334 theorems are true and they stay; they stop being independent claims and become these laws' verification tables. Every theorem here computes BOTH sides in the kernel — the quantity walked, the law evaluated from the factorisation — so the identity is what is decided, never a literal restating the walk. THE BRIDGE IS THE ONE WORTH READING: max_unit_order (Wave) and unit_group_exponent (the Fermat wing) are the same function, the Carmichael lambda, computed in two wings that never cite each other. Their agreement is Carmichael's theorem — the exponent of the unit group is ATTAINED by a single element — and it is decided here rather than assumed. CLAIMED: the tabulated identities over the moduli named, each decided by the kernel over its own finite domain, axiom-free. NOT CLAIMED: any of these laws for all m, which quantifies over an infinite domain and cannot be asked of `by decide` at all. The tables are evidence for the laws, never proofs of them, and the frontier is in the theorem names. — held by [divisor_count_law_mod_2](/theorem/divisor_count_law_mod_2) and its 352 siblings below.

**353 theorems**, from [divisor_count_law_mod_2](/theorem/divisor_count_law_mod_2) onward, each proven `by decide` in <a href="/lean/Laws.lean">lean/Laws.lean</a>, axiom-free against the bare Lean kernel. This article is computed from the ledger — nothing here is authored, and every claim carries its citation. 176 of its 353 theorems seal a BOUNDARY rather than a capability — naming what the model does not do, where it fails, or what it excludes — starting with [idempotent_count_law_mod_2](/theorem/idempotent_count_law_mod_2). A boundary stated here is decided.

**[Re-prove this wing in your browser ↗](https://live.lean-lang.org/#project=mathlib-stable&url=https%3A%2F%2Fraw.githubusercontent.com%2Fuuidna%2Fuuidna%2Frefs%2Fheads%2Fmain%2Flean%2FLaws.lean)** — nothing to install. The editor fetches `lean/Laws.lean` from the repository and re-decides all 353 proofs on Lean v4.33.0, the toolchain this ledger is sealed against. The wing imports nothing, so what the reader runs is the whole input: a green run there is the reader's own verdict, not ours.

### THE DIVISOR COUNT IS THE PRODUCT OF (e + 1), at m = 2. The kernel walks every d ≤ 2 testing divisibility, and separately multiplies (e + 1) over 2's prime exponents [[2,1]]; they agree at 2. A divisor is an independent choice of exponent between 0 and e at each prime, so the count is a product of ranges — that is the whole reason, and Wave.lean's 37 divisors_of_*_number_* theorems are this identity evaluated 37 times without it. It is also a SPEED law: the walk is O(m), the product is O(number of primes).
The ledger holds this as [divisor_count_law_mod_2](/theorem/divisor_count_law_mod_2) — proven `by decide`, sorry-free:

```lean
([[0,1,2]].map (fun c => c.countP (fun x => x != 0 && 2 % x == 0))).foldl (· + ·) 0 = lawDivisors [(2,1)]
```

### THE DIVISOR COUNT IS THE PRODUCT OF (e + 1), at m = 53. The kernel walks every d ≤ 53 testing divisibility, and separately multiplies (e + 1) over 53's prime exponents [[53,1]]; they agree at 2. A divisor is an independent choice of exponent between 0 and e at each prime, so the count is a product of ranges — that is the whole reason, and Wave.lean's 37 divisors_of_*_number_* theorems are this identity evaluated 37 times without it. It is also a SPEED law: the walk is O(m), the product is O(number of primes).
The ledger holds this as [divisor_count_law_mod_53](/theorem/divisor_count_law_mod_53) — proven `by decide`, sorry-free:

### EULER'S TOTIENT AS A PRODUCT, at m = 2. The units are walked by gcd; the law multiplies p^(e−1)(p−1) over the factorisation. Both give 1. CRT splits the unit group across prime powers, and at p^e the non-units are exactly the multiples of p. Wave.lean seals 25 of these as bare numbers; this generates them, and it turns an O(m log m) walk into an O(sqrt m) product.
The ledger holds this as [euler_phi_law_mod_2](/theorem/euler_phi_law_mod_2) — proven `by decide`, sorry-free:

```lean
([[0,1]].map (fun c => c.countP (fun x => Nat.gcd x 2 == 1))).foldl (· + ·) 0 = lawPhi [(2,1)]
```

### THE IDEMPOTENTS NUMBER 2^omega(m), at m = 2. x² = x is walked directly; the law is two raised to the count of DISTINCT primes — 1 here, giving 2. The exponents do not matter, only how many primes there are: an idempotent is a free choice of 0 or 1 in each prime-power component, so the count doubles per prime and is blind to multiplicity. That blindness is the content — it is why 12 and 18 and 24 all have four.
The ledger holds this as [idempotent_count_law_mod_2](/theorem/idempotent_count_law_mod_2) — proven `by decide`, sorry-free:

```lean
([[0,1]].map (fun c => c.countP (fun x => (x * x) % 2 == x))).foldl (· + ·) 0 = lawIdempotents [(2,1)]
```

### THE NILPOTENTS NUMBER m / rad(m), at m = 2. A residue is nilpotent exactly when every prime of 2 divides it, so the nilpotents are the multiples of the radical 2 and there are 1 of them. The walk searches for a vanishing power; the law divides 2 by the product of its distinct primes. Wave.lean seals 37 of these counts and states none of this.
The ledger holds this as [nilpotent_count_law_mod_2](/theorem/nilpotent_count_law_mod_2) — proven `by decide`, sorry-free:

```lean
([[0,1]].map (fun c => c.countP (fun x => pw x 2 2 == 0))).foldl (· + ·) 0 = 2 / lawRadical [(2,1)]
```

### THE UNITS SUM TO ZERO, at m = 3. The residues coprime to 3 add to 0 mod 3, because x is a unit exactly when 3 − x is and the two cancel; with 3 > 2 no unit is its own partner, so the pairing is perfect and the sum is forced. Wave.lean carries 37 separate theorems each asserting this zero at one modulus. The exclusion of m = 2 is the hypothesis rather than an inconvenience: there the only unit is 1, it has no distinct partner, and the sum is 1.
The ledger holds this as [unit_sum_vanishes_mod_3](/theorem/unit_sum_vanishes_mod_3) — proven `by decide`, sorry-free:

```lean
(([[0,1,2]].map (fun c => (c.filter (fun x => Nat.gcd x 3 == 1)).foldl (fun a x => a + x) 0)).foldl (· + ·) 0) % 3 = 0
```

### CARMICHAEL AT m = 2, IN BOTH HALVES. The closed form gives lambda(2) = 1 — the lcm over 2's prime powers of p^(e−1)(p−1), with 2 the one place the pattern breaks (1, then 2, then 2^(e−2)). FIRST HALF: every one of the 1 units satisfies a^1 = 1, so 1 kills the group. SECOND HALF: the unit 1 has order exactly 1, so the bound is ATTAINED and nothing smaller would do. Together those are the definition of the group's exponent, and that it is attained by a single element is Carmichael's theorem — true here, and not automatic for abelian groups in general. THIS IS ALSO THE SPEED LAW: computing lambda by folding every element's order walks 1 units for up to 2 steps each; killing the group costs log 1 steps per unit and attainment costs ONE walk. Wave.lean's max_unit_order_mod_* family is this number, sealed 37 times without the closed form that generates it.
The ledger holds this as [carmichael_exponent_is_attained_mod_2](/theorem/carmichael_exponent_is_attained_mod_2) — proven `by decide`, sorry-free:

```lean
([[1]].all (fun c => c.all (fun a => pw a (lawLambda [(2,1)]) 2 == 1))) ∧ (ordOf 1 2 == lawLambda [(2,1)])
```
